### Failed love attempts

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=72 "Edit section: Failed love attempts")]

See also: [Apollo and Daphne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_and_Daphne "Apollo and Daphne")

Love affairs ascribed to Apollo are a late development in Greek mythology.[\[390\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-392) Their vivid anecdotal qualities have made some of them favorites of painters since the Renaissance, the result being that they stand out more prominently in the modern imagination.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Apollo_and_Daphne_%28Bernini%29.jpg/220px-Apollo_and_Daphne_%28Bernini%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Apollo_and_Daphne_\(Bernini\).jpg)

*[Apollo and Daphne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_and_Daphne "Apollo and Daphne")* by [Bernini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernini "Bernini") in the [Galleria Borghese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galleria_Borghese "Galleria Borghese")

[Daphne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daphne "Daphne") was a [nymph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymph "Nymph") who scorned Apollo's advances and ran away from him. When Apollo chased her in order to persuade her, she changed herself into a laurel tree. According to other versions, she cried for help during the chase, and [Gaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaia "Gaia") helped her by taking her in and placing a laurel tree in her place.[\[391\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-393) According to Roman poet [Ovid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovid "Ovid"), the chase was brought about by [Cupid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupid "Cupid"), who hit Apollo with a golden arrow of love and Daphne with a leaden arrow of hatred.[\[392\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-394) The myth explains the origin of the [laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_Laurel "Bay Laurel") and the connection of Apollo with the laurel and its leaves, which his priestess employed at [Delphi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphi "Delphi"). The leaves became the symbol of victory and laurel wreaths were given to the victors of the [Pythian games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythian_games "Pythian games").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

[Marpessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marpessa_\(daughter_of_Evenus\) "Marpessa (daughter of Evenus)") was kidnapped by [Idas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idas "Idas") but was loved by Apollo as well. [Zeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeus "Zeus") made her choose between them, and she chose Idas on the grounds that Apollo, being immortal, would tire of her when she grew old.[\[393\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-395)

[Sinope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinope_\(mythology\) "Sinope (mythology)"), a nymph, was approached by the amorous Apollo. She made him promise that he would grant to her whatever she would ask for, and then cleverly asked him to let her stay a virgin. Apollo kept his promise and went back.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

[Bolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolina "Bolina") was admired by Apollo but she refused him and jumped into the sea. To avoid her death, Apollo turned her into a nymph, saving her life.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

[Castalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castalia "Castalia") was a [nymph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymph "Nymph") whom Apollo loved. She fled from him and dove into the [spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castalian_Spring "Castalian Spring") at Delphi, at the base of [Mt. Parnassos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mt._Parnassos "Mt. Parnassos"), which was then named after her. Water from this spring was sacred; it was used to clean the Delphian temples and inspire the priestesses.[\[394\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-396)

[Cassandra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassandra "Cassandra") was a daughter of Hecuba and Priam. Apollo wished to court her. Cassandra promised to return his love on one condition – he should give her the power to see the future. Apollo fulfilled her wish, but she went back on her word and rejected him soon after. Angered that she broke her promise, Apollo cursed her that even though she would see the future, no one would ever believe her prophecies.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

The [Sibyl of Cumae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumaean_Sibyl "Cumaean Sibyl") like Cassandra promised Apollo her love if he would give her a boon. The Sibyl took a handful of sand and asked Apollo to grant her years of life as many as the grains of sand she held in her hands. Apollo granted her wish, but Sibyl went back on her word. Although Sibyl did live an extended life as Apollo had promised, he did not give her agelessness along with it, so she shrivelled and shrank and only her voice remained.[\[395\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-397)

[Hestia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hestia "Hestia"), the goddess of the hearth, rejected both Apollo's and Poseidon's marriage proposals and swore that she would always stay unmarried.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

In one version of the prophet [Tiresias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiresias "Tiresias")'s origins, he was originally a woman who promised Apollo to sleep with him if he would give her music lessons. Apollo gave her her wish, but then she went back on her word and refused him. Apollo in anger turned her into a man.[\[396\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-398)

### Female counterparts

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=73 "Edit section: Female counterparts")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Gavin_Hamilton_-_Apollo_and_Artemis%2C_1770.jpg/200px-Gavin_Hamilton_-_Apollo_and_Artemis%2C_1770.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gavin_Hamilton_-_Apollo_and_Artemis,_1770.jpg)

Apollo and Artemis, by [Gavin Hamilton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavin_Hamilton_\(artist\) "Gavin Hamilton (artist)")

#### Artemis

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=74 "Edit section: Artemis")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c7/Apollo_Artemis_Brygos_Louvre_G151.jpg/220px-Apollo_Artemis_Brygos_Louvre_G151.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Apollo_Artemis_Brygos_Louvre_G151.jpg)

Apollo (left) and [Artemis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artemis "Artemis"), by [Brygos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brygos "Brygos") (potter signed). Tondo of an Attic red-figure cup c. 470 BC, [Musée du Louvre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mus%C3%A9e_du_Louvre "Musée du Louvre").

Artemis as the sister of Apollo, is *thea apollousa*, that is, she as a female divinity represented the same idea that Apollo did as a male divinity. In the pre-Hellenic period, their relationship was described as the one between husband and wife, and there seems to have been a tradition which actually described Artemis as the wife of Apollo.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*] However, this relationship was never sexual but spiritual,[\[397\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-399) which is why they both are seen being unmarried in the [Hellenic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenic_period "Hellenic period").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

Artemis, like her brother, is armed with a bow and arrows. She is the cause of sudden deaths of women. She also is the protector of the young, especially girls. Though she has nothing to do with oracles, music or poetry, she sometimes led the female chorus on Olympus while Apollo sang.[\[398\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-400) The laurel (*[daphne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daphne "Daphne")*) was sacred to both. *Artemis Daphnaia* had her temple among the Lacedemonians, at a place called Hypsoi.[\[399\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-401) *Apollo Daphnephoros* had a temple in [Eretria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eretria "Eretria"), a "place where the citizens are to take the oaths".[\[400\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-402) In later times when Apollo was regarded as identical with the sun or [Helios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helios "Helios"), Artemis was naturally regarded as [Selene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selene "Selene") or the moon.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

#### Hecate

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=75 "Edit section: Hecate")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Jusepe_de_Ribera_-_Hecate%2C_Procession_to_a_Witches%27_Sabbath.jpg/220px-Jusepe_de_Ribera_-_Hecate%2C_Procession_to_a_Witches%27_Sabbath.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jusepe_de_Ribera_-_Hecate,_Procession_to_a_Witches%27_Sabbath.jpg)

Hecate: procession to witches' sabbath, by [Jusepe de Ribera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jusepe_de_Ribera "Jusepe de Ribera")

[Hecate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hecate "Hecate"), the goddess of witchcraft and magic, is the chthonic counterpart of Apollo. They both are cousins, since their mothers – Leto and Asteria – are sisters. One of Apollo's epithets, *Hecatos*, is the masculine form of Hecate, and both names mean "working from afar". While Apollo presided over the prophetic powers and magic of light and heaven, Hecate presided over the prophetic powers and magic of night and chthonian darkness.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*] If Hecate is the "gate-keeper", Apollo *Agyieus* is the "door-keeper". Hecate is the goddess of crossroads and Apollo is the god and protector of streets.[\[401\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ReferenceB-403)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/Houbraken%2C_Arnold_-_Pallas_Athene_Visiting_Apollo_on_the_Parnassus_-_1703.jpeg/220px-Houbraken%2C_Arnold_-_Pallas_Athene_Visiting_Apollo_on_the_Parnassus_-_1703.jpeg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Houbraken,_Arnold_-_Pallas_Athene_Visiting_Apollo_on_the_Parnassus_-_1703.jpeg)

Pallas Athena visiting Apollo on Parnassus, by [Arnold Houbraken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnold_Houbraken "Arnold Houbraken")

The oldest evidence found for Hecate's worship is at Apollo's temple in Miletos. There, Hecate was taken to be Apollo's sister counterpart in the absence of Artemis.[\[401\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ReferenceB-403) Hecate's lunar nature makes her the goddess of the waning moon and contrasts and complements, at the same time, Apollo's solar nature.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

#### Athena

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=76 "Edit section: Athena")]

As a deity of knowledge and great power, Apollo was seen being the male counterpart of [Athena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athena "Athena"). Being Zeus' favorite children, they were given more powers and duties. Apollo and Athena often took up the role of protectors of cities, and were patrons of some of the important cities. Athena was the principal goddess of [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens "Athens"), Apollo was the principal god of [Sparta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparta "Sparta").[\[402\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-404)

As patrons of arts, Apollo and Athena were companions of the [Muses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muses "Muses"), the former a much more frequent companion than the latter.[\[403\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-405) Apollo was sometimes called the son of Athena and Hephaestus.[\[404\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-406)

In the Trojan War, as Zeus' executive, Apollo is seen holding the [aegis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegis "Aegis") like Athena usually does.[\[405\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-407) Apollo's decisions were usually approved by his sister Athena, and they both worked to establish the law and order set forth by Zeus.[\[406\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-408)

### Apollo in the *Oresteia*

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=77 "Edit section: Apollo in the Oresteia")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Over_life-size_statue_of_Apollo_Citharoedus_%28the_lyre-player%29%2C_from_the_Gymnasium_of_Salamis%2C_2nd_century_AD%2C_Cyprus_Museum%2C_Nicosia%2C_Cyprus_%2822484277762%29.jpg/220px-Over_life-size_statue_of_Apollo_Citharoedus_%28the_lyre-player%29%2C_from_the_Gymnasium_of_Salamis%2C_2nd_century_AD%2C_Cyprus_Museum%2C_Nicosia%2C_Cyprus_%2822484277762%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Over_life-size_statue_of_Apollo_Citharoedus_\(the_lyre-player\),_from_the_Gymnasium_of_Salamis,_2nd_century_AD,_Cyprus_Museum,_Nicosia,_Cyprus_\(22484277762\).jpg)

Statue of Apollo Cithaeroedus, [Cyprus Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyprus_Museum "Cyprus Museum"), [Nicosia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicosia "Nicosia")

In [Aeschylus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeschylus "Aeschylus")' *[Oresteia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oresteia "Oresteia")* trilogy, [Clytemnestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clytemnestra "Clytemnestra") kills her husband, King [Agamemnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agamemnon "Agamemnon") because he had sacrificed their daughter [Iphigenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iphigenia "Iphigenia") to proceed forward with the Trojan war. Apollo gives an order through the Oracle at Delphi that Agamemnon's son, [Orestes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orestes "Orestes"), is to kill Clytemnestra and [Aegisthus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegisthus "Aegisthus"), her lover. Orestes and Pylades carry out the revenge, and consequently Orestes is pursued by the [Erinyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erinyes "Erinyes") or Furies (female personifications of [vengeance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenge "Revenge")).\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

Apollo and the Furies argue about whether the [matricide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matricide "Matricide") was justified; Apollo holds that the bond of marriage is sacred and Orestes was avenging his father, whereas the Erinyes say that the bond of blood between mother and son is more meaningful than the bond of marriage. They invade his temple, and he drives them away. He says that the matter should be brought before Athena. Apollo promises to protect Orestes, as Orestes has become Apollo's [supplicant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplication "Supplication"). Apollo advocates Orestes at the trial, and ultimately Athena rules in favor of Apollo.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

### Roman Apollo

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=78 "Edit section: Roman Apollo")]

The Roman worship of Apollo was adopted from the Greeks.[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreese1911185-409) As a quintessentially [Greek god](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Greek_deities "List of Greek deities"), Apollo had no direct Roman equivalent, although later Roman poets often referred to him as **Phoebus**.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*] There was a tradition that the Delphic oracle was consulted as early as the period of the [kings of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Kingdom "Roman Kingdom") during the reign of [Tarquinius Superbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarquinius_Superbus "Tarquinius Superbus").[\[408\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-410)

On the occasion of a pestilence in the 430s BCE, Apollo's [first temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Apollo_Sosianus "Temple of Apollo Sosianus") at Rome was established in the Flaminian fields, replacing an older cult site there known as the "Apollinare".[\[409\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-411) During the [Second Punic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Punic_War "Second Punic War") in 212 BCE, the *[Ludi Apollinares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludi_Apollinares "Ludi Apollinares")* ("Apollonian Games") were instituted in his honor, on the instructions of a prophecy attributed to one Marcius.[\[410\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-412) In the time of [Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus "Augustus"), who considered himself under the special protection of Apollo and was even said to be his son, his worship developed and he became one of the chief gods of Rome.[\[411\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-413)[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreese1911185-409)

After the [Battle of Actium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Actium "Battle of Actium"), which was fought near a sanctuary of Apollo, Augustus enlarged Apollo's temple, dedicated a portion of the spoils to him, and instituted [quinquennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quinquennial "Quinquennial") games in his honour.[\[412\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-414) He also erected [a new temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Apollo_\(Palatine\) "Temple of Apollo (Palatine)") to the god on the [Palatine hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palatine_Hill "Palatine Hill").[\[413\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-415) Sacrifices and prayers on the Palatine to Apollo and [Diana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_\(mythology\) "Diana (mythology)") formed the culmination of the [Secular Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_Games "Secular Games"), held in 17 BCE to celebrate the dawn of a new era.[\[414\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-416)

## Festivals

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=79 "Edit section: Festivals")]

The chief Apollonian festival was the [Pythian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythian_Games "Pythian Games") held every four years at Delphi and was one of the four great [Panhellenic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panhellenic_Games "Panhellenic Games"). Also of major importance was the [Delia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delia_\(festival\) "Delia (festival)") held every four years on Delos. Athenian annual festivals included the [Boedromia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boedromia "Boedromia"), [Metageitnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metageitnia "Metageitnia"),[\[415\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-417) [Pyanepsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyanepsia "Pyanepsia"), and [Thargelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thargelia "Thargelia"). Spartan annual festivals were the [Carneia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carneia "Carneia") and the [Hyacinthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyacinthia "Hyacinthia"). [Thebes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thebes,_Greece "Thebes, Greece") every nine years held the [Daphnephoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daphnephoria "Daphnephoria").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

## Attributes and symbols

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=80 "Edit section: Attributes and symbols")]

Apollo's most common attributes were the bow and [arrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow "Arrow"). Other attributes of his included the [kithara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kithara "Kithara") (an advanced version of the common [lyre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyre "Lyre")), the [plectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plectrum "Plectrum") and the sword. Another common emblem was the [sacrificial tripod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrificial_tripod "Sacrificial tripod"), representing his prophetic powers. The [Pythian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythian_Games "Pythian Games") were held in Apollo's honor every four years at [Delphi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphi "Delphi"). The [bay laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_laurel "Bay laurel") plant was used in expiatory sacrifices and in making the [crown of victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurel_wreath "Laurel wreath") at these games.[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreese1911185-409)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Ai-Khanoum-gold_stater_of_Antiochos1.jpg/250px-Ai-Khanoum-gold_stater_of_Antiochos1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ai-Khanoum-gold_stater_of_Antiochos1.jpg)

Gold stater of the [Seleucid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucid "Seleucid") king [Antiochus I Soter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_I_Soter "Antiochus I Soter") (reigned 281–261 BCE), showing on the reverse a nude Apollo holding his key attributes: two arrows and a bow

The [palm tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_tree "Palm tree") was also sacred to Apollo because he had been born under one in [Delos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delos "Delos"). Animals sacred to Apollo included [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf "Wolf"), dolphins, [roe deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_deer "Roe deer"), [swans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swan "Swan"), [cicadas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cicada "Cicada") (symbolizing music and song), [ravens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raven "Raven"), [hawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawk "Hawk"), [crows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corvus_\(genus\) "Corvus (genus)") (Apollo had hawks and crows as his messengers),[\[416\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Porphyry-418) snakes (referencing Apollo's function as the god of prophecy), mice and [griffins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griffin "Griffin"), mythical eagle–lion hybrids of Eastern origin.[\[407\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreese1911185-409)

[Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer "Homer") and [Porphyry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porphyry_\(philosopher\) "Porphyry (philosopher)") wrote that Apollo had a hawk as his messenger.[\[417\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-419)[\[416\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Porphyry-418) In many myths Apollo is transformed into a hawk.[\[418\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-420)[\[419\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-421)[\[420\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-422) In addition, [Claudius Aelianus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudius_Aelianus "Claudius Aelianus") wrote that in [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptians "Egyptians") people believed that hawks were sacred to the god[\[421\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Aelian_10.14-423) and that according to the ministers of Apollo in Egypt there were certain men called "hawk-keepers" (ἱερακοβοσκοί) who fed and tended the hawks belonging to the god.[\[422\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-424) [Eusebius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eusebius "Eusebius") wrote that the second appearance of the moon is held sacred in the city of Apollo in Egypt and that the city's symbol is a man with a hawklike face ([Horus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horus "Horus")).[\[423\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-425) [Claudius Aelianus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudius_Aelianus "Claudius Aelianus") wrote that Egyptians called Apollo [Horus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horus "Horus") in their own language.[\[421\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Aelian_10.14-423)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Apolocitaredo8.jpg/250px-Apolocitaredo8.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Apolocitaredo8.jpg)

*[Apollo Citharoedus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Citharoedus "Apollo Citharoedus")* ("Apollo with a kithara"), [Musei Capitolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musei_Capitolini "Musei Capitolini"), Rome

As god of colonization,[\[424\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-426) Apollo gave oracular guidance on colonies, especially during the height of colonization, 750–550 BCE. According to Greek tradition, he helped [Cretan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crete "Crete") or [Arcadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcadia_\(ancient_region\) "Arcadia (ancient region)") colonists found the city of [Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy "Troy"). However, this story may reflect a cultural influence which had the reverse direction: [Hittite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites "Hittites") [cuneiform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneiform_script "Cuneiform script") texts mention an Asia Minor god called *Appaliunas* or *Apalunas* in connection with the city of [Wilusa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilusa "Wilusa") attested in Hittite inscriptions, which is now generally regarded as being identical with the Greek [Ilion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy "Troy") by most scholars. In this interpretation, Apollo's title of *Lykegenes* can simply be read as "born in Lycia", which effectively severs the god's supposed link with wolves (possibly a [folk etymology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_etymology "Folk etymology")).\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

In literary contexts, Apollo represents harmony, order, and reason—characteristics contrasted with those of [Dionysus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionysus "Dionysus"), god of wine, who represents ecstasy and disorder. The contrast between the roles of these gods is reflected in the adjectives [Apollonian and Dionysian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollonian_and_Dionysian "Apollonian and Dionysian"). However, the Greeks thought of the two qualities as complementary: the two gods are brothers, and when Apollo at winter left for [Hyperborea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperborea "Hyperborea"), he would leave the Delphic oracle to Dionysus. This contrast appears to be shown on the two sides of the [Borghese Vase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borghese_Vase "Borghese Vase").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

Apollo is often associated with the [Golden Mean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_mean_\(philosophy\) "Golden mean (philosophy)"). This is the Greek [ideal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideal_\(ethics\) "Ideal (ethics)") of [moderation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderation "Moderation") and a [virtue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtue "Virtue") that opposes [gluttony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gluttony "Gluttony").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

In antiquity, Apollo was associated with the planet Mercury. The ancient Greeks believed that Mercury as observed during the morning was a different planet than the one during the evening, because each twilight Mercury would appear farther from the Sun as it set than it had the night before. The morning planet was called Apollo, and the one at evening Hermes/Mercury before they realised they were the same, thereupon the name 'Mercury/Hermes' was kept, and 'Apollo' was dropped.[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:merc-1)

## Apollo in the arts

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=81 "Edit section: Apollo in the arts")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/%CE%9A%CE%AF%CE%BF%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82_%CE%91%CF%80%CF%8C%CE%BB%CE%BB%CF%89%CE%BD%CE%B1_-_Apolo_en_la_columna_-_Apollo_in_the_column.jpg/220px-%CE%9A%CE%AF%CE%BF%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82_%CE%91%CF%80%CF%8C%CE%BB%CE%BB%CF%89%CE%BD%CE%B1_-_Apolo_en_la_columna_-_Apollo_in_the_column.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%CE%9A%CE%AF%CE%BF%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82_%CE%91%CF%80%CF%8C%CE%BB%CE%BB%CF%89%CE%BD%CE%B1_-_Apolo_en_la_columna_-_Apollo_in_the_column.jpg)

Statue of Apollo at the [Academy of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_Athens_\(modern\) "Academy of Athens (modern)")

Apollo is a common theme in Greek and Roman art and also in the art of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance "Renaissance"). The earliest Greek word for a statue is "delight" (ἄγαλμα, *agalma*), and the sculptors tried to create forms which would inspire such guiding vision. [Maurice Bowra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Bowra "Maurice Bowra") notices that the Greek artist puts into a god the highest degree of power and beauty that can be imagined. The sculptors derived this from observations on human beings, but they also embodied in concrete form, issues beyond the reach of ordinary thought.[\[425\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-427)

The naked bodies of the statues are associated with the cult of the body which was essentially a religious activity.[\[426\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-428) The muscular frames and limbs combined with slim waists indicate the Greek desire for health, and the physical capacity which was necessary in the hard Greek environment.[\[427\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-429) The statues of Apollo and the other gods present them in their full youth and strength. "In the balance and relation of their limbs, such figures express their whole character, mental and physical, and reveal their central being, the radiant reality of youth in its heyday".[\[428\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-430)

### Archaic sculpture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=82 "Edit section: Archaic sculpture")]

Numerous free-standing statues of male youths from [Archaic Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_Greece "Archaic Greece") exist, and were once thought to be representations of Apollo, though later discoveries indicated that many represented mortals.[\[429\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-431) In 1895, V. I. Leonardos proposed the term *[kouros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kouros "Kouros")* ("male youth") to refer to those from [Keratea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keratea "Keratea"); this usage was later expanded by Henri Lechat in 1904 to cover all statues of this format.[\[430\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-432)[\[431\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-433)

The earliest examples of life-sized statues of Apollo may be two figures from the [Ionic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionians "Ionians") sanctuary on the island of [Delos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delos "Delos"). Such statues were found across the Greek-speaking world, the preponderance of these were found at the sanctuaries of Apollo with more than one hundred from the sanctuary of *Apollo Ptoios*, [Boeotia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeotia "Boeotia") alone.[\[432\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-434) Significantly more rare are the life-sized bronze statues. One of the few originals which survived into the present day—so rare that its discovery in 1959 was described as "a miracle" by Ernst Homann-Wedeking—is the masterpiece bronze, *[Piraeus Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus_Apollo "Piraeus Apollo")*. It was found in [Piraeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piraeus "Piraeus"), a [port city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_city "Port city") close to Athens, and is believed to have come from north-eastern [Peloponnesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peloponnesus "Peloponnesus"). It is the only surviving large-scale Peloponnesian statue.[\[433\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-435)

### Classical sculpture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=83 "Edit section: Classical sculpture")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Apollon_de_Mantoue_Louvre_MA689.jpg/180px-Apollon_de_Mantoue_Louvre_MA689.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Apollon_de_Mantoue_Louvre_MA689.jpg)

[Apollo of Mantua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_of_Mantua "Apollo of Mantua"), marble Roman copy after a 5th-century-BCE Greek original attributed to [Polykleitos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polykleitos "Polykleitos"), Musée du Louvre

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Runeberg_ateneum_apollon_ja_marsyas.jpg/180px-Runeberg_ateneum_apollon_ja_marsyas.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Runeberg_ateneum_apollon_ja_marsyas.jpg)

Marble sculpture of Apollo and [Marsyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsyas "Marsyas") by [Walter Runeberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Runeberg "Walter Runeberg"), at the arrivals hall of [Ateneum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ateneum "Ateneum") in Helsinki, [Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finland "Finland")

The famous [Apollo of Mantua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_of_Mantua "Apollo of Mantua") and its variants are early forms of the [Apollo Citharoedus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Citharoedus "Apollo Citharoedus") statue type, in which the god holds the [cithara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cithara "Cithara"), a sophisticated seven-stringed variant of the lyre, in his left arm. While none of the Greek originals have survived, several Roman copies from approximately the late 1st or early 2nd century exist, of which an example is the [Apollo Barberini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Barberini "Apollo Barberini").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

### Hellenistic Greece-Rome

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=84 "Edit section: Hellenistic Greece-Rome")]

Apollo as a handsome beardless young man, is often depicted with a cithara (as Apollo Citharoedus) or bow in his hand, or reclining on a tree (the [Apollo Lykeios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Lykeios "Apollo Lykeios") and [Apollo Sauroctonos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Sauroctonos "Apollo Sauroctonos") types). The [Apollo Belvedere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_Belvedere "Apollo Belvedere") is a [marble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marble "Marble") sculpture that was rediscovered in the late 15th century; for centuries it epitomized the ideals of [Classical Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Antiquity "Classical Antiquity") for Europeans, from the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance "Renaissance") through the 19th century. The marble is a [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Greece "Hellenistic Greece") or Roman copy of a bronze original by the Greek sculptor [Leochares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leochares "Leochares"), made between 330 and 320 BCE.[\[434\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-436)

The life-size so-called "[Adonis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adonis "Adonis")" found in 1780 on the site of a *[villa suburbana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_villa "Roman villa")* near the [Via Labicana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Labicana "Via Labicana") in the Roman suburb of Centocelle is identified as an Apollo by modern scholars. In the late 2nd century CE floor mosaic from [El Djem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Djem "El Djem"), Roman *Thysdrus*, he is identifiable as [Apollo Helios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helios "Helios") by his effulgent [halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halo_\(religious_iconography\) "Halo (religious iconography)"), though now even a god's divine [nakedness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nudity "Nudity") is concealed by his cloak, a mark of increasing conventions of modesty in the later [Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire "Roman Empire").\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

Another haloed Apollo in mosaic, from [Hadrumentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrumentum "Hadrumentum"), is in the museum at [Sousse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sousse "Sousse").[\[435\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-437) The conventions of this representation, head tilted, lips slightly parted, large-eyed, curling [hair cut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hairstyle "Hairstyle") in locks grazing the neck, were developed in the 3rd century BCE to depict [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great").[\[436\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-438) Some time after this mosaic was executed, the earliest depictions of Christ would also be beardless and haloed.\[*[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]

## Modern reception

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=85 "Edit section: Modern reception")]

Apollo often appears in [modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernity "Modernity") and [popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology_in_popular_culture "Greek mythology in popular culture") due to his status as the god of music, dance and poetry.

### Postclassical art and literature

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=86 "Edit section: Postclassical art and literature")]

#### Dance and music

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=87 "Edit section: Dance and music")]

Apollo has featured in dance and music in modern culture. [Percy Bysshe Shelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percy_Bysshe_Shelley "Percy Bysshe Shelley") composed a "Hymn of Apollo" (1820), and the god's instruction of the Muses formed the subject of [Igor Stravinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Stravinsky "Igor Stravinsky")'s *[Apollon musagète](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollon_musag%C3%A8te "Apollon musagète")* (1927–1928). In 1978, the Canadian band [Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rush_\(band\) "Rush (band)") released [an album](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemispheres_\(Rush_album\) "Hemispheres (Rush album)") with songs ["Apollo: Bringer of Wisdom"/"Dionysus: Bringer of Love"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cygnus_X-1_Book_II "Cygnus X-1 Book II").[\[437\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-439)

#### Books

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=88 "Edit section: Books")]

Apollo has been portrayed in modern literature, such as when [Charles Handy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Handy "Charles Handy") in *Gods of Management* (1978) uses Greek gods as a metaphor to portray various types of [organizational culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organizational_culture "Organizational culture"). Apollo represents a "role" culture where order, reason, and [bureaucracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucracy "Bureaucracy") prevail.[\[438\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-440)

### Psychology and philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=89 "Edit section: Psychology and philosophy")]

See also: [Apollonian and Dionysian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollonian_and_Dionysian "Apollonian and Dionysian") and [Apollo archetype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_archetype "Apollo archetype")

In the philosophical discussion of the arts, a distinction is sometimes made between the [Apollonian and Dionysian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollonian_and_Dionysian "Apollonian and Dionysian") impulses, where the former is concerned with imposing intellectual order and the latter with chaotic creativity. [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche "Friedrich Nietzsche") argued that a fusion of the two was most desirable.[\[439\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-441) Psychologist [Carl Jung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Jung "Carl Jung")'s [Apollo archetype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_archetype "Apollo archetype") represents what he saw as the disposition in people to over-intellectualise and maintain emotional distance.[\[440\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-442)

### Spaceflight

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Apollo\&action=edit\&section=90 "Edit section: Spaceflight")]

See also: [Apollo program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program "Apollo program")

In spaceflight, the 1960s and 1970s [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA") program for orbiting and landing astronauts on the Moon was named after [Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program "Apollo program"), by [NASA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA "NASA") manager [Abe Silverstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abe_Silverstein "Abe Silverstein"):

> Apollo riding his chariot across the Sun was appropriate to the grand scale of the proposed program.[\[441\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pressrelease-443)
>
> — Abe Silverstein, Release 69-36

1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-2)** [Attic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attic_Greek "Attic Greek"), [Ionic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_Greek "Ionic Greek"), [Homeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeric_Greek "Homeric Greek") and [Koinē Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koin%C4%93_Greek_language "Koinē Greek language"): Ἀπόλλων, romanized: *Apóllōn*, genitive: Ἀπόλλωνος, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Ancient_Greek "Romanization of Ancient Greek"): *Apóllōnos* [Attic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attic_Greek "Attic Greek")–[Ionic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionic_Greek "Ionic Greek") pronunciation: [\[a.pól.lɔːn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Greek "Help:IPA/Greek"), [\[a.pól.lɔː.nos\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Greek "Help:IPA/Greek"); [Koinē Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koin%C4%93_Greek_language "Koinē Greek language"): [\[aˈpol.lon\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA "Help:IPA"), [\[aˈpol.lo.nos\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin "Help:IPA/Latin")\
   [Doric Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doric_Greek_language "Doric Greek language"): Ἀπέλλων, romanized: *Apéllōn*, [Doric Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doric_Greek_language "Doric Greek language") pronunciation: [\[a.pel.lɔ̂ːn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA "Help:IPA"); [Arcadocypriot Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcadocypriot_Greek "Arcadocypriot Greek"):: Ἀπείλων, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Ancient_Greek "Romanization of Ancient Greek"): *Apeílōn*, Arcadocypriot Greek: [\[a.pěː.lɔːn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Greek "Help:IPA/Greek"); [Aeolic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeolic_Greek_language "Aeolic Greek language"): Ἄπλουν, romanized: *Áploun*, [Aeolic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeolic_Greek_language "Aeolic Greek language"): [\[á.ploːn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA "Help:IPA")\
   [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language "Latin language"): *Apollō*, genitive: *Apollinis*, [Classical Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Latin_language "Classical Latin language"): [\[äˈpɔlːʲoː\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin "Help:IPA/Latin"), [\[äˈpɔl.lʲɪ.nɪs̠\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin "Help:IPA/Latin"); [Late Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Latin "Late Latin"): [\[ɑˈpɔl.lɔ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin "Help:IPA/Latin"), [\[ɑˈpɔl.li.nis\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Latin "Help:IPA/Latin")
2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-4)** *Mousike* (the art of the Muses) was an integral part of life in the ancient Greek world, and the term covered not only music but also dance, lyrics, theatre and the performance of poetry.

443. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-445)** According to [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer "Homer"), *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad "Iliad")* [1.570–579](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg001.perseus-eng1:1.570), [14.338](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg001.perseus-eng1:14.338), *[Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odyssey "Odyssey")* [8.312](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg002.perseus-eng1:8.312), Hephaestus was apparently the son of Hera and Zeus, see Gantz, p. 74.
444. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-446)** According to [Hesiod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesiod "Hesiod"), *[Theogony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theogony "Theogony")* [927–929](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Hes.+Th.+927), Hephaestus was produced by Hera alone, with no father, see Gantz, p. 74.
445. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-447)** According to [Hesiod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesiod "Hesiod")'s *[Theogony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theogony "Theogony")* [886–890](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Hes.+Th.+886), of Zeus' children by his seven wives, Athena was the first to be conceived, but the last to be born; Zeus impregnated Metis then swallowed her, later Zeus himself gave birth to Athena "from his head", see Gantz, pp. 51–52, 83–84.
446. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-448)** According to [Hesiod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesiod "Hesiod"), *[Theogony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theogony "Theogony")* [183–200](https://www.perseus.tufts.edu/hopper/text?doc=Hes.+Th.+183), Aphrodite was born from Uranus' severed genitals, see Gantz, pp. 99–100.
447. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-449)** According to [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer "Homer"), Aphrodite was the daughter of Zeus (*[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad "Iliad")* [3.374](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg001.perseus-eng1:3.374), [20.105](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg001.perseus-eng1:20.105); *[Odyssey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odyssey "Odyssey")* [8.308](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg002.perseus-eng1:8.308), [320](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg002.perseus-eng1:8.320)) and Dione (*[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad "Iliad")* [5.370–71](http://data.perseus.org/citations/urn:cts:greekLit:tlg0012.tlg001.perseus-eng1:5.370)), see Gantz, pp. 99–100.
